Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Gildong is playing a video game called Block Adventure. In Block Adventure, there are n columns of blocks in a row, and the columns are numbered from 1 to n. All blocks have equal heights. The height of the i-th column is represented as h_i, which is the number of blocks stacked in the i-th column.

Gildong plays the game as a character that can stand only on the top of the columns. At the beginning, the character is standing on the top of the 1-st column. The goal of the game is to move the character to the top of the n-th column.

The character also has a bag that can hold infinitely many blocks. When the character is on the top of the i-th column, Gildong can take one of the following three actions as many times as he wants: 

  * if there is at least one block on the column, remove one block from the top of the i-th column and put it in the bag; 
  * if there is at least one block in the bag, take one block out of the bag and place it on the top of the i-th column; 
  * if i < n and |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k, move the character to the top of the i+1-st column. k is a non-negative integer given at the beginning of the game. Note that it is only possible to move to the next column. 



In actions of the first two types the character remains in the i-th column, and the value h_i changes.

The character initially has m blocks in the bag. Gildong wants to know if it is possible to win the game. Help Gildong find the answer to his question.

Input

Each test contains one or more test cases. The first line contains the number of test cases t (1 ≤ t ≤ 1000). Description of the test cases follows.

The first line of each test case contains three integers n, m, and k (1 ≤ n ≤ 100, 0 ≤ m ≤ 10^6, 0 ≤ k ≤ 10^6) — the number of columns in the game, the number of blocks in the character's bag at the beginning, and the non-negative integer k described in the statement.

The second line of each test case contains n integers. The i-th integer is h_i (0 ≤ h_i ≤ 10^6), the initial height of the i-th column.

Output

For each test case, print "YES" if it is possible to win the game. Otherwise, print "NO".

You can print each letter in any case (upper or lower).

Example

Input

5
3 0 1
4 3 5
3 1 2
1 4 7
4 10 0
10 20 10 20
2 5 5
0 11
1 9 9
99


Output

YES
NO
YES
NO
YES

Note

In the first case, Gildong can take one block from the 1-st column, move to the 2-nd column, put the block on the 2-nd column, then move to the 3-rd column.

In the second case, Gildong has to put the block in his bag on the 1-st column to get to the 2-nd column. But it is impossible to get to the 3-rd column because |h_2 - h_3| = 3 > k and there is no way to decrease the gap.

In the fifth case, the character is already on the n-th column from the start so the game is won instantly.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` columns of blocks, with heights `h_i` for column i
2. We start at column 1, want to reach column n
3. We can:
   - Remove a block from current column (decrease h_i) and add to bag
   - Add a block from bag to current column (increase h_i)
   - Move to next column (i+1) only if |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k
4. We start with `m` blocks in the bag
5. We need to determine if we can reach the last column

## Key Insights

1. We can always decrease the height of any column (by taking blocks) without limit
2. We can increase height only if we have blocks in our bag
3. To move from column i to i+1, we need |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k
4. Since we can reduce heights, we can always make h_i smaller to satisfy the condition when moving forward
5. The challenge is whether we can "create" enough blocks in our bag to make the transitions work

## Strategy

The key insight is that we can work backwards from the end. For each column i, we need to be able to reach column i+1.

To move from column i to i+1:
- If |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k, we can move directly
- If |h_i - h_{i+1}| > k, we need to adjust either h_i or h_{i+1}

Since we can only reduce heights and add blocks from our bag, we can:
- Decrease h_i to make |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k
- Increase h_{i+1} using blocks from our bag (but we need to have blocks available)

However, note that when we move from i to i+1, we can only use blocks that were already collected or are available in our bag.

- h2=11, so we need h1 ≥ 11-5 = 6
- We start with h1=0, so we need to add blocks to make h1=6
- We need 6 blocks, but we only have 5 blocks in bag → cannot do it → NO ✓

Third example: "4 10 0\n10 20 10 20"

k=0 means we need identical heights between consecutive columns.

We need:
- h1 = h2
- h2 = h3  
- h3 = h4

So h1=h2=h3=h4.

Currently: 10,20,10,20

We can change column 1 to 20: need to add 10 blocks to column 1 (uses 10 blocks from bag) → bag becomes 0, h1=20

Then column 2 is 20, so h2=20, h3=10, need h2=h3 → 20≠10

We can reduce column 2 from 20 to 10: remove 10 blocks from column 2, bag becomes 0+10=10

Now h2=10, h3=10 → good

Then from col3 to col4: h3=10, h4=20, need 10=20 → not equal

We need to make h3=20, which requires 10 blocks, but bag is 10, so we can do it.

After adding 10 blocks to col3, h3=20, bag becomes 0

Now from col3 to col4: |20-20|=0 ≤ 0 → good

So sequence:
- Start: col1=10, col2=20, col3=10, col4=20, bag=10
- Add 10 blocks to col1 → col1=20, bag=0  
- Reduce col2 by 10 → col2=10, bag=10
- Reduce col3 by 10 → col3=0, bag=20? No

When we reduce col2 from 20 to 10, we remove 10 blocks, which adds to bag: bag=0+10=10
When we reduce col3 from 10 to 0, we add 10 to bag, bag=10+10=20
Then add 20 blocks to col3 to make it 20? That would require 20 blocks, but we only have 20, so we can do it.

But we only need to make col3=20, so we need to add 10 blocks (since it was 10).

After reducing col3 from 10 to 0: col3=0, bag=10+10=20
Then add 10 blocks to col3: col3=10, bag=10
Then we need col3=20, so add 10 more: col3=20, bag=0

But we have col4=20, so |20-20|=0, good.

But we need to go from col3 to col4 after making col3=20.

However, we only have 10 blocks in bag at the end, not enough to make col3=20 from 10.

We need to go from col3 to col4 when col3=20, col4=20 → good.

But to get col3=20 from col3=10, we need to add 10 blocks.

We have bag=0 after adding to col3 and reducing it? Let's track:

- Initially: col1=10, col2=20, col3=10, col4=20, bag=10
- Add 10 to col1: col1=20, bag=0
- Reduce col2 by 10: col2=10, bag=0+10=10
- Reduce col3 by 10: col3=0, bag=10+10=20
- Add 10 to col3: col3=10, bag=10
- Add 10 to col3: col3=20, bag=0

Now we can move from col3 to col4: |20-20|=0 ≤ 0 → good.

So it is possible.

In the transition from col2 to col3, after adjusting, we need |h2 - h3| = 0.

After setting h2=10, h3=10, it works.

For each transition, we need to ensure we can achieve the required height in the next column.

In the algorithm, for each transition i→i+1:
- We need |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k
- We can achieve any h_i in [0, h_i_current + bag]
- We need h_i in [h_{i+1} - k, h_{i+1} + k] ∩ [0, h_i_current + bag]
- This is non-empty if h_{i+1} - k ≤ h_i_current + bag

And after adjustment, the bag changes.

In the example with k=0, for transition from i to i+1:
- We need h_i = h_{i+1}
- So we need h_i to be in [h_{i+1}, h_{i+1}] (since k=0)
- We also need h_i ≤ h_i_current + bag
- So we need h_{i+1} ≤ h_i_current + bag

And then if we set h_i = h_{i+1}, the bag change is (h_i_current - h_{i+1}) if h_i_current > h_{i+1}, or (h_{i+1} - h_i_current) if h_i_current < h_{i+1} (but this would require negative, so only if h_i_current > h_{i+1}).

Actually, if we set h_i_new = h_{i+1}, then:
- If h_i_current > h_{i+1}: bag changes by (h_i_current - h_{i+1}) (we remove blocks from column i, add to bag)
- If h_i_current < h_{i+1}: bag changes by (h_{i+1} - h_i_current) (we add blocks to column i, remove from bag)

In the example with [10,20,10,20], k=0:

Transition 0→1 (col1 to col2: h2=20):
- Need 20 ≤ 10 + 10 → 20 ≤ 20, true
- Set h1_new = 20, bag_new = 10 + (10-20) = 0

Transition 1→2 (col2 to col3: h3=10):
- Need 10 ≤ 20 + 0 → 10 ≤ 20, true
- Set h2_new = 10, bag_new = 0 + (20-10) = 10

Transition 2→3 (col3 to col4: h4=20):
- Need 20 ≤ 10 + 10 → 20 ≤ 20, true
- Set h3_new = 20, bag_new = 10 + (10-20) = 0

All transitions work → YES ✓

So the algorithm is:

```python
def solve():
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        return "YES"
    
    bag = m
    current_height = h[0]
    
    for i in range(n - 1):
        # Need to transition from column i to i+1
        # Can we achieve a height h_i_new such that |h_i_new - h[i+1]| <= k?
        # h_i_new should be in [max(0, h[i+1] - k), min(h[i+1] + k, current_height + bag)] and also in [0, current_height + bag]
        # The intersection is non-empty if max(0, h[i+1] - k) <= current_height + bag
        if h[i+1] - k <= current_height + bag:
            # We can make the transition
            # Choose the best h_i_new to minimize bag change
            # Target interval: [max(0, h[i+1] - k), min(h[i+1] + k, current_height + bag)]
            low = max(0, h[i+1] - k)
            high = min(h[i+1] + k, current_height + bag)
            
            # If current_height is in [low, high], we can keep it
            if current_height >= low and current_height <= high:
                # No change to bag
                pass
            elif current_height < low:
                # Need to increase height to at least low
                h_new = low
                # Remove (current_height - h_new) blocks? No
                # We are going from current_height to h_new, which is higher
                # So we need to add (h_new - current_height) blocks to the column
                # This removes (h_new - current_height) from our bag
                bag -= (h_new - current_height)
                current_height = h_new
            else:  # current_height > high
                # Need to decrease height to at most high
                h_new = high
                # We are going from current_height to h_new, which is lower
                # So we remove (current_height - h_new) blocks from the column, which adds to our bag
                bag += (current_height - h_new)
                current_height = h_new
        else:
            return "NO"
    
    return "YES"
```

But wait, in the case where current_height < low, we are setting h_new = low, and we need to add (low - current_height) blocks to column i, which reduces our bag by (low - current_height).

Similarly, when current_height > high, we set h_new = high, and we remove (current_height - high) blocks from column i, which increases our bag by (current_height - high).

This matches.

Let me verify with the examples.

i=0: from col0 to col1, h[1]=3
- h[1]-k = 3-1=2, current_height=4, bag=0, so 2 <= 4+0 → true
- low = max(0,2)=2, high = min(3+1, 4+0)=min(4,4)=4
- current_height=4 is in [2,4] → no change, bag=0, height=4

i=1: from col1 to col2, h[2]=5
- h[2]-k=5-1=4, current_height=4, bag=0, 4 <= 4+0 → true
- low = max(0,4)=4, high = min(6,4)=4
- current_height=4 is in [4,4] → no change, bag=0, height=4

- current_height=1 < 2, so set h_new=2
- bag = 1 - (2-1) = 0

Final code:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        print("YES")
        continue
        
    bag = m
    current_height = h[0]
    
    for i in range(n - 1):
        # Check if we can transition from column i to i+1
        if h[i+1] - k <= current_height + bag:
            low = max(0, h[i+1] - k)
            high = min(h[i+1] + k, current_height + bag)
            
            if current_height >= low and current_height <= high:
                # No change needed
                pass
            elif current_height < low:
                # Need to increase height to at least low
                h_new = low
                bag -= (h_new - current_height)
                current_height = h_new
            else:  # current_height > high
                # Need to decrease height to at most high
                h_new = high
                bag += (current_height - h_new)
                current_height = h_new
        else:
            print("NO")
            break
    else:
        print("YES")
```

Note: The `else` clause of the for loop is executed only if we didn't break, so we print "YES" if all transitions are successful.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Gildong is playing a video game called Block Adventure. In Block Adventure, there are n columns of blocks in a row, and the columns are numbered from 1 to n. All blocks have equal heights. The height of the i-th column is represented as h_i, which is the number of blocks stacked in the i-th column.

Gildong plays the game as a character that can stand only on the top of the columns. At the beginning, the character is standing on the top of the 1-st column. The goal of the game is to move the character to the top of the n-th column.

The character also has a bag that can hold infinitely many blocks. When the character is on the top of the i-th column, Gildong can take one of the following three actions as many times as he wants: 

  * if there is at least one block on the column, remove one block from the top of the i-th column and put it in the bag; 
  * if there is at least one block in the bag, take one block out of the bag and place it on the top of the i-th column; 
  * if i < n and |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k, move the character to the top of the i+1-st column. k is a non-negative integer given at the beginning of the game. Note that it is only possible to move to the next column. 



In actions of the first two types the character remains in the i-th column, and the value h_i changes.

The character initially has m blocks in the bag. Gildong wants to know if it is possible to win the game. Help Gildong find the answer to his question.

Input

Each test contains one or more test cases. The first line contains the number of test cases t (1 ≤ t ≤ 1000). Description of the test cases follows.

The first line of each test case contains three integers n, m, and k (1 ≤ n ≤ 100, 0 ≤ m ≤ 10^6, 0 ≤ k ≤ 10^6) — the number of columns in the game, the number of blocks in the character's bag at the beginning, and the non-negative integer k described in the statement.

The second line of each test case contains n integers. The i-th integer is h_i (0 ≤ h_i ≤ 10^6), the initial height of the i-th column.

Output

For each test case, print "YES" if it is possible to win the game. Otherwise, print "NO".

You can print each letter in any case (upper or lower).

Example

Input

5
3 0 1
4 3 5
3 1 2
1 4 7
4 10 0
10 20 10 20
2 5 5
0 11
1 9 9
99


Output

YES
NO
YES
NO
YES

Note

In the first case, Gildong can take one block from the 1-st column, move to the 2-nd column, put the block on the 2-nd column, then move to the 3-rd column.

In the second case, Gildong has to put the block in his bag on the 1-st column to get to the 2-nd column. But it is impossible to get to the 3-rd column because |h_2 - h_3| = 3 > k and there is no way to decrease the gap.

In the fifth case, the character is already on the n-th column from the start so the game is won instantly.

Actually, we don't need to do both. We just need to close the gap to ≤ k.

We can either:
- Reduce `h_i` until it's ≤ `h_{i+1} + k`
- Increase `h_{i+1}` until it's ≥ `h_i - k`

The required change is:
- If `h_i > h_{i+1} + k`: we need to remove `(h_i - (h_{i+1} + k))` blocks from `h_i`
- If `h_{i+1} > h_i + k`: we need to add `(h_{i+1} - (h_i + k))` blocks to `h_{i+1}`

So the blocks needed for transition `i` to `i+1` is:
- `max(0, h_i - h_{i+1} - k)` if `h_i > h_{i+1} + k`
- `max(0, h_{i+1} - h_i - k)` if `h_{i+1} > h_i + k`

Actually, it's `max(0, h_i - h_{i+1} - k)` when `h_i > h_{i+1} + k`, and `max(0, h_{i+1} - h_i - k)` when `h_{i+1} > h_i + k`.

But these are the same in magnitude. The total blocks needed is `max(0, |h_i - h_{i+1}| - k)`.

Wait - no. If `h_i > h_{i+1}`, we need to reduce by `h_i - h_{i+1} - k` blocks. If `h_i < h_{i+1}`, we need to add by `h_{i+1} - h_i - k` blocks.

So the blocks needed is `max(0, |h_i - h_{i+1}| - k)`.

And we can achieve this if we have at least that many blocks in our bag.

Therefore, the algorithm is:
1. If we're already at column n, return YES
2. For each transition from column i to i+1:
   - Calculate blocks needed = max(0, |h[i] - h[i+1]| - k)
   - If blocks needed > current bag count, return NO
   - Otherwise, update bag count: bag_count -= blocks_needed
3. If we successfully reach the end, return YES

As before, for fixed S, we can achieve r - a ≥ -m if -S ≥ -m, i.e., S ≤ m.

Therefore, we need that there exists S in [L, U] such that S ≤ m, where:
- L = max(0, h_i - h_{i+1} - k)
- U = max(0, h_i - h_{i+1} + k)  # if h_i - h_{i+1} + k < 0, then U = 0, and we need L ≤ U = 0, so h_i - h_{i+1} - k ≤ 0

The interval [L, U] is non-empty if and only if L ≤ U.

L ≤ U means:
- if h_i - h_{i+1} - k ≤ 0, then L = 0, U = max(0, h_i - h_{i+1} + k), so we need 0 ≤ U, which is always true if U≥0
- if h_i - h_{i+1} - k > 0, then L = h_i - h_{i+1} - k, U = max(0, h_i - h_{i+1} + k), and we need h_i - h_{i+1} - k ≤ max(0, h_i - h_{i+1} + k)

This is true because if h_i - h_{i+1} - k > 0, then h_i - h_{i+1} + k > h_i - h_{i+1} - k, so the upper bound is larger.

Also, we need that for some S in [L, U], S ≤ m.

This is true if and only if L ≤ m.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Gildong is playing a video game called Block Adventure. In Block Adventure, there are n columns of blocks in a row, and the columns are numbered from 1 to n. All blocks have equal heights. The height of the i-th column is represented as h_i, which is the number of blocks stacked in the i-th column.

Gildong plays the game as a character that can stand only on the top of the columns. At the beginning, the character is standing on the top of the 1-st column. The goal of the game is to move the character to the top of the n-th column.

The character also has a bag that can hold infinitely many blocks. When the character is on the top of the i-th column, Gildong can take one of the following three actions as many times as he wants: 

  * if there is at least one block on the column, remove one block from the top of the i-th column and put it in the bag; 
  * if there is at least one block in the bag, take one block out of the bag and place it on the top of the i-th column; 
  * if i < n and |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k, move the character to the top of the i+1-st column. k is a non-negative integer given at the beginning of the game. Note that it is only possible to move to the next column. 



In actions of the first two types the character remains in the i-th column, and the value h_i changes.

The character initially has m blocks in the bag. Gildong wants to know if it is possible to win the game. Help Gildong find the answer to his question.

Input

Each test contains one or more test cases. The first line contains the number of test cases t (1 ≤ t ≤ 1000). Description of the test cases follows.

The first line of each test case contains three integers n, m, and k (1 ≤ n ≤ 100, 0 ≤ m ≤ 10^6, 0 ≤ k ≤ 10^6) — the number of columns in the game, the number of blocks in the character's bag at the beginning, and the non-negative integer k described in the statement.

The second line of each test case contains n integers. The i-th integer is h_i (0 ≤ h_i ≤ 10^6), the initial height of the i-th column.

Output

For each test case, print "YES" if it is possible to win the game. Otherwise, print "NO".

You can print each letter in any case (upper or lower).

Example

Input

5
3 0 1
4 3 5
3 1 2
1 4 7
4 10 0
10 20 10 20
2 5 5
0 11
1 9 9
99


Output

YES
NO
YES
NO
YES

Note

In the first case, Gildong can take one block from the 1-st column, move to the 2-nd column, put the block on the 2-nd column, then move to the 3-rd column.

In the second case, Gildong has to put the block in his bag on the 1-st column to get to the 2-nd column. But it is impossible to get to the 3-rd column because |h_2 - h_3| = 3 > k and there is no way to decrease the gap.

In the fifth case, the character is already on the n-th column from the start so the game is won instantly.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` columns of blocks, with heights `h_i` for column i
2. Character starts at column 1 and wants to reach column n
3. Three allowed actions:
   - Remove one block from current column and add to bag
   - Add one block from bag to current column
   - Move to next column if `|h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k`
4. We start with `m` blocks in the bag
5. We need to determine if we can reach the last column

## Key Insights

1. We can only move from column i to i+1 if `|h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k`
2. We can modify heights using blocks in our bag
3. To move from column i to i+1, we need to ensure the height difference is at most k
4. If we have blocks in our bag, we can both reduce heights (by removing blocks) and increase heights (by adding blocks)
5. The key constraint is that we can't create blocks out of nowhere - we can only add blocks from our bag

## Strategy

The core insight is that we can only move from column i to i+1 if we can make `|h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k`. 

We can:
- Remove blocks from column i to decrease its height
- Add blocks to column i to increase its height (using our bag)

However, since we start with a limited number of blocks in our bag, we need to be strategic.

The key observation is that we can simulate the entire journey from left to right. For each transition from column i to i+1:

1. If `|h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k`, we can move directly
2. If `|h_i - h_{i+1}| > k`, we need to adjust either column i or column i+1 using our bag

But we can only add blocks to columns using the bag, and we start with m blocks.

Wait, let me reconsider: we can remove blocks from any column and put them in our bag, which increases our bag capacity. So we can effectively "create" more blocks by removing from other columns.

Actually, the bag allows us to:
- Remove blocks from current column → add to bag (increasing bag capacity)
- Add blocks from bag to current column (decreasing bag capacity)

So we can both decrease column heights and increase them, but the total number of blocks is conserved (except for the bag which can hold arbitrarily many).

The key constraint is that we need to ensure we can make transitions between adjacent columns with height differences at most k.

For each transition from i to i+1:
- If `|h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k`, we can move directly
- If `|h_i - h_{i+1}| > k`, we need to adjust the heights

To reduce the difference, we can:
- Remove blocks from the taller column (making it shorter)
- Add blocks to the shorter column (making it taller)

But we can only add blocks if we have them in our bag. We can remove blocks from any column to get more blocks in our bag.

However, we need to be careful about the sequence. We can only modify the current column when we're on it.

## Algorithm

We can process columns from left to right:

1. Start at column 1, with m blocks in bag
2. For each transition from column i to i+1:
   - If current difference is ≤ k, we can move
   - If current difference is > k, we need to make adjustments
   - We can either:
     - Remove blocks from column i to reduce its height (this gives us more blocks in bag)
     - Add blocks to column i+1 to increase its height (using blocks from bag)

But we need to ensure we don't run out of blocks.

Actually, let me think differently. The height changes are adjustments we can make. 

For transition from i to i+1:
- We want `|h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k`
- We can decrease h_i (by removing blocks from column i)
- We can increase h_i+1 (by adding blocks from bag)

The minimum number of blocks we need to add to column i+1 is `max(0, h_i+1 - h_i + k)` to make the difference ≤ k when h_i+1 is larger.

Similarly, if h_i > h_i+1, we might need to decrease h_i by removing blocks.

Actually, the net effect is that we need to ensure the difference is at most k. We can always reduce the taller column by removing blocks (which gives us more blocks to use in the bag), or we can increase the shorter column by using blocks from our bag.

The constraint is that we can't create blocks out of nowhere, so we need to have enough blocks in our bag to make adjustments.

Wait, I'm overcomplicating. The key insight is that we can simulate the entire path and track how many blocks we need to add or remove.

For each transition from i to i+1:
- Current difference: `abs(h_i - h_{i+1})`
- If this difference is ≤ k, we can move
- If this difference is > k, we need to adjust

We can:
- Increase h_{i+1} by adding blocks from bag → this reduces the difference
- Decrease h_i by removing blocks → this also reduces the difference and gives us more blocks in bag

Actually, decreasing h_i by removing blocks gives us more blocks in bag, which is beneficial.

So the greedy approach:
1. Start with m blocks in bag
2. For each transition from i to i+1:
   - If we can move directly (difference ≤ k), continue
   - Otherwise, we need to make adjustments
   - To reduce the difference, we can either increase the lower height or decrease the higher height
   - We should prefer decreasing the higher height (since it gives us more blocks in our bag)

Let me formalize:

For transition from i to i+1:
- Let diff = abs(h_i - h_{i+1})
- If diff ≤ k: move to next column
- If diff > k:
  - We can reduce the taller height by removing blocks from that column
  - This removes blocks from the column, adding to our bag
  - We can increase the shorter height by adding blocks from bag to the shorter column

But the net change in blocks in bag: if we decrease a taller column, we gain blocks in bag.

Actually, let's think about the net effect:

For transition from i to i+1 to be possible, we need to ensure that the difference can be reduced to ≤ k.

The minimum blocks needed in our bag to make the transition is:
- If h_i > h_{i+1} and h_i - h_{i+1} > k: we need to reduce h_i by (h_i - h_{i+1} - k) blocks
- If h_i < h_{i+1} and h_{i+1} - h_i > k: we need to increase h_{i+1} by (h_{i+1} - h_i - k) blocks

The net block change:
- In the first case: we remove (h_i - h_{i+1} - k) blocks from column i → add to bag → our bag increases by (h_i - h_{i+1} - k)
- In the second case: we add (h_{i+1} - h_i - k) blocks to column i+1 → our bag decreases by (h_{i+1} - h_i - k)

So our bag changes accordingly.

We need to track the bag size throughout the journey.

## Algorithm

1. Initialize bag = m
2. For each i from 1 to n-1:
   - If we can move from i to i+1 (i.e. |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k), continue
   - Otherwise, calculate the adjustment needed
   - Update the bag size accordingly
   - If bag becomes negative, return "NO"
3. If we successfully reach column n, return "YES"

Let me implement this:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    bag = m
    
    # Try to move from each column to next
    possible = True
    for i in range(n - 1):
        diff = abs(h[i] - h[i + 1])
        
        if diff <= k:
            # Can move directly
            continue
        else:
            # Need to adjust
            if h[i] > h[i + 1]:
                # h[i] is too tall, need to reduce it by (h[i] - h[i+1] - k)
                blocks_to_remove = h[i] - h[i + 1] - k
                if bag < blocks_to_remove:
                    possible = False
                    break
                bag -= blocks_to_remove
                # After removing, h[i] becomes h[i] - blocks_to_remove
                # But we don't need to update h[i] since we're just tracking bag
                # Actually, we need to update h[i] for future transitions
                h[i] = h[i] - blocks_to_remove
            else:
                # h[i+1] is too tall, need to increase it by (h[i+1] - h[i] - k)
                blocks_to_add = h[i + 1] - h[i] - k
                if bag < blocks_to_add:
                    possible = False
                    break
                bag -= blocks_to_add
                # h[i+1] becomes h[i+1] + blocks_to_add
                h[i + 1] = h[i + 1] + blocks_to_add
    
    if possible:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

Actually, let me reconsider the bag changes:

- When we remove blocks from a column, we add them to our bag → bag increases
- When we add blocks to a column, we remove them from our bag → bag decreases

```python

                # Removing blocks increases our bag

                h[i] -= blocks_to_remove

                # h[i+1] is too tall, increase it by (h[i+1] - h[i] - k)
                blocks_to_add = h[i + 1] - h[i] - k
                # Adding blocks decreases our bag
```

   - If difference ≤ k, we can move directly
   - If difference > k:

       b) Increase the shorter column → lose (difference - k) blocks in bag

So for transition i to i+1:
- If |h[i] - h[i+1]| > k:
  - Let excess = |h[i] - h[i+1]| - k
  - If we reduce the taller column, we gain excess blocks in our bag
  - So bag += excess
  - Update the height of the taller column to h[i] - excess (or h[i+1] - excess)

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    bag = m
    
    possible = True
    for i in range(n - 1):
        diff = abs(h[i] - h[i + 1])
        
        if diff <= k:
            # Can move directly
            continue
        else:
            # Need to adjust - reduce the excess
            excess = diff - k
            
            # Reduce the taller column by excess
            if h[i] > h[i + 1]:
                # Reduce h[i]
                h[i] -= excess
                bag += excess
            else:
                # Reduce h[i+1]
                h[i + 1] -= excess
                bag += excess
    
    print("YES")
```

So when going from column i to i+1, we can only modify column i (by removing blocks or adding blocks).

We cannot modify column i+1 at this point.

Therefore, to move from i to i+1, we must ensure that the difference after our actions on column i is ≤ k.

We can:
- Remove blocks from column i → increases our bag
- Add blocks to column i → decreases our bag

The goal is to make |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k after our modifications.

- We can change h_i to any value from 0 to h_i by removing blocks
- We can add blocks to h_i up to our bag capacity

Position i has height h_i initially.
After modifications, let new_h_i be in [0, h_i] if we only remove, or in [h_i, h_i + bag] if we add.

We want |new_h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k.

We need to find if there exists a new_h_i such that |new_h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k and the net effect on our bag is valid.

The net effect:
- If we remove r blocks from h_i, we get r blocks in bag, new_h_i = h_i - r
- If we add a blocks to h_i, we lose a blocks from bag, new_h_i = h_i + a

So net change in bag: if we remove r and add a, net change = r - a

But we can only remove what's in the column, and only add what's in the bag.

The question is: is there a new_h_i such that:
- |new_h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k
- new_h_i ≥ 0
- new_h_i ≤ h_i + bag (because we can add at most bag blocks)

And if new_h_i < h_i, we get (h_i - new_h_i) blocks in bag
If new_h_i > h_i, we lose (new_h_i - h_i) blocks from bag

So we need to check if there's a value in the range [max(0, h_{i+1} - k), min(h_i, h_{i+1} + k)] that is achievable given the bag constraints.

The achievable values for new_h_i are in [0, h_i + bag].

We need the intersection of [max(0, h_{i+1} - k), min(h_i, h_{i+1} + k)] and [0, h_i + bag] to be non-empty.

But we also need to consider that if new_h_i > h_i, we lose (new_h_i - h_i) blocks from bag, so we need to have enough in bag to cover that.

However, since we are at this column, we can only use the current bag, and we may have increased it by removing from this column.

The net effect is that we can achieve any value in the range [0, h_i + bag], because:
- To increase a column, we need blocks in bag
- To decrease a column, we get blocks in bag

The catch is that we can't create blocks in the column, only from removing from it.

For a given new_h_i, the net change in bag is:
- If new_h_i < h_i: change = h_i - new_h_i (positive)
- If new_h_i > h_i: change = - (new_h_i - h_i) (negative)

So the final bag after this transition is: bag + (h_i - new_h_i) if new_h_i < h_i, or bag - (new_h_i - h_i) if new_h_i > h_i.

We need this to be >= 0 for future transitions.

But we can choose new_h_i to minimize the bag usage.

To maximize our future flexibility, we should try to minimize the net block usage or increase the bag.

The net block change is:
- If new_h_i < h_i: net = h_i - new_h_i
- If new_h_i > h_i: net = - (new_h_i - h_i)

We want to find if there exists new_h_i such that:
- new_h_i in [max(0, h_{i+1} - k), min(h_i, h_{i+1} + k)] 
- and new_h_i in [0, h_i + bag] (achievable)
- and the net change is such that bag + net_change >= 0

Actually, net_change is always >= - (h_i - 0) = -h_i, and we start with bag, so as long as we don't go negative, it should be ok.

But we need to ensure that for the chosen new_h_i, the required blocks are available.

Since we can only add blocks from our bag, if new_h_i > h_i, we need to have at least (new_h_i - h_i) blocks in our bag at this point.

If new_h_i < h_i, we get (h_i - new_h_i) blocks in our bag.

So the algorithm for each transition i to i+1:

1. Find the range of new_h_i that satisfies |new_h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k
   - This is [h_{i+1} - k, h_{i+1} + k] intersected with [0, h_i + bag] and [0, h_i] for the upper bound if we only remove, but we can also add.

Actually, new_h_i can be in [0, h_i + bag].

The constraints are:
- new_h_i >= max(0, h_{i+1} - k)
- new_h_i <= min(h_i + bag, h_{i+1} + k)

And |new_h_i - h_{i+1}| <= k is already in the bounds.

So the range is [L, R] where:
- L = max(0, h_{i+1} - k)
- R = min(h_i + bag, h_{i+1} + k)

If L > R, then no solution, return "NO"

Otherwise, we can choose any new_h_i in [L, R].

For the net change in bag:
- If we choose new_h_i <= h_i: net_change = h_i - new_h_i >= 0
- If we choose new_h_i > h_i: net_change = - (new_h_i - h_i) <= 0

To maximize our future flexibility, we should choose new_h_i to minimize the net loss or maximize gain.

If we can choose new_h_i < h_i, then net_change >= 0, which is beneficial.

So if there exists new_h_i in [L, min(h_i, R)] (i.e. new_h_i <= h_i), then net_change >= 0.

If not, then we must have new_h_i > h_i, and net_change = - (new_h_i - h_i) >= - (R - h_i)

So the final bag becomes: bag + net_change = bag - (new_h_i - h_i)

We need this to be >= 0, so bag >= new_h_i - h_i

Since new_h_i > h_i, new_h_i - h_i > 0.

The minimum new_h_i in the range where new_h_i > h_i is the first value > h_i, which is h_i + min(1, R - h_i) etc.

To ensure feasibility, we need to check if there's a choice that doesn't make bag go negative.

The safest choice is to choose new_h_i as close to h_i as possible from below, if possible.

So algorithm:

For each transition from i to i+1:
- L = max(0, h[i+1] - k)
- R = min(h[i] + bag, h[i+1] + k)
- If L > R: return "NO"
- Otherwise, if there exists new_h_i in [L, min(h[i], R)]:
  - Then we can choose new_h_i <= h[i], net_change >= 0
  - bag becomes bag + (h[i] - new_h_i) >= bag
- else: (L > h[i] and R > h[i])
  - We must choose new_h_i > h[i]
  - We need to choose new_h_i in (h[i], R]
  - To minimize net loss, choose new_h_i = h[i] + 1 (minimum above h[i])
  - net_change = - (new_h_i - h[i]) = -1
  - bag becomes bag - 1
  - But we can choose any new_h_i in (h[i], R], and the net change is - (new_h_i - h[i])
  - To ensure bag >= 0, we need bag >= (new_h_i - h[i]) for our choice
  - Since we can choose new_h_i to be as close to h[i] as possible, the minimum net loss is when new_h_i = h[i] + 1, so we need bag >= 1
  - If bag >= 1, we can choose new_h_i = h[i] + 1, then net_change = -1, bag becomes bag - 1
  - If bag < 1, then no choice is possible, return "NO"

Actually, we need to ensure that for the chosen new_h_i, the net change doesn't make bag negative.

The minimum net change when new_h_i > h_i is when new_h_i is as small as possible, which is h[i] + 1.

So if L > h[i], we need bag >= 1 to be able to choose new_h_i = h[i] + 1.

If L <= h[i], we can choose new_h_i = max(L, h[i]) if max(L, h[i]) <= min(h[i], R), or choose new_h_i = h[i] if possible.

To be safe, we can always choose new_h_i = max(L, h[i]) if max(L, h[i]) <= R.

But if max(L, h[i]) > R, then no solution.

So the condition is:
- If L > R: "NO"
- Else if max(L, h[i]) > R: "NO"
- Else: we can always choose new_h_i = max(L, h[i]) (which is <= R), and if new_h_i <= h[i], net_change = h[i] - new_h_i >= 0, bag increases
- If new_h_i > h[i], net_change = - (new_h_i - h[i]) >= - (R - h[i])

We only need to ensure net_change doesn't make bag negative.

Since net_change is negative only when new_h_i > h[i], and in that case, the net change is - (new_h_i - h[i]) >= - (R - h[i])

The minimum net change is when new_h_i is as large as possible, which is R, net_change = - (R - h[i])

- If L > h[i]: net_change = - (L - h[i])

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    bag = m
    possible = True
    
    for i in range(n - 1):
        # Target for new_h_i
        L = max(0, h[i+1] - k)
        R = min(h[i] + bag, h[i+1] + k)
        
        if L > R:
            possible = False
            break
            
        if L <= h[i]:
            # We can choose new_h_i = L (since L <= h[i])
            # net_change = h[i] - L (positive)
            bag = bag + (h[i] - L)
        else:
            # L > h[i], must choose new_h_i = L
            # net_change = - (L - h[i]) (negative)
            net_change = - (L - h[i])
            if bag < net_change:  # bag + net_change would be negative
                possible = False
                break
            bag = bag + net_change
    
    if possible:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    bag = m
    possible = True
    
    for i in range(n - 1):
        # Target for new_h_i
        L = max(0, h[i+1] - k)
        R = min(h[i] + bag, h[i+1] + k)
        
        if L > R:
            possible = False
            break
            
        if L <= h[i]:
            # We can choose new_h_i = L (since L <= h[i])
            # net_change = h[i] - L (positive)
            bag = bag + (h[i] - L)
        else:
            # L > h[i], must choose new_h_i = L
            # net_change = - (L - h[i]) (negative)
            net_change = - (L - h[i])
            if bag < net_change:  # bag + net_change would be negative
                possible = False
                break
            bag = bag + net_change
    
    if possible:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Gildong is playing a video game called Block Adventure. In Block Adventure, there are n columns of blocks in a row, and the columns are numbered from 1 to n. All blocks have equal heights. The height of the i-th column is represented as h_i, which is the number of blocks stacked in the i-th column.

Gildong plays the game as a character that can stand only on the top of the columns. At the beginning, the character is standing on the top of the 1-st column. The goal of the game is to move the character to the top of the n-th column.

The character also has a bag that can hold infinitely many blocks. When the character is on the top of the i-th column, Gildong can take one of the following three actions as many times as he wants: 

  * if there is at least one block on the column, remove one block from the top of the i-th column and put it in the bag; 
  * if there is at least one block in the bag, take one block out of the bag and place it on the top of the i-th column; 
  * if i < n and |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k, move the character to the top of the i+1-st column. k is a non-negative integer given at the beginning of the game. Note that it is only possible to move to the next column. 



In actions of the first two types the character remains in the i-th column, and the value h_i changes.

The character initially has m blocks in the bag. Gildong wants to know if it is possible to win the game. Help Gildong find the answer to his question.

Input

Each test contains one or more test cases. The first line contains the number of test cases t (1 ≤ t ≤ 1000). Description of the test cases follows.

The first line of each test case contains three integers n, m, and k (1 ≤ n ≤ 100, 0 ≤ m ≤ 10^6, 0 ≤ k ≤ 10^6) — the number of columns in the game, the number of blocks in the character's bag at the beginning, and the non-negative integer k described in the statement.

The second line of each test case contains n integers. The i-th integer is h_i (0 ≤ h_i ≤ 10^6), the initial height of the i-th column.

Output

For each test case, print "YES" if it is possible to win the game. Otherwise, print "NO".

You can print each letter in any case (upper or lower).

Example

Input

5
3 0 1
4 3 5
3 1 2
1 4 7
4 10 0
10 20 10 20
2 5 5
0 11
1 9 9
99


Output

YES
NO
YES
NO
YES

Note

In the first case, Gildong can take one block from the 1-st column, move to the 2-nd column, put the block on the 2-nd column, then move to the 3-rd column.

In the second case, Gildong has to put the block in his bag on the 1-st column to get to the 2-nd column. But it is impossible to get to the 3-rd column because |h_2 - h_3| = 3 > k and there is no way to decrease the gap.

In the fifth case, the character is already on the n-th column from the start so the game is won instantly.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` columns of blocks, with heights `h_i` for column `i`
2. We start at column 1 and want to reach column `n`
3. We can:
   - Remove a block from current column and add to bag
   - Add a block from bag to current column
   - Move to next column if `|h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k`
4. We start with `m` blocks in the bag
5. We need to determine if we can reach the final column

## Key Insights

1. We can adjust the height of any column by removing or adding blocks (using the bag)
2. When moving from column `i` to `i+1`, we need `|h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k`
3. We can make the height of column `i` any value we want by adding or removing blocks, as long as we have enough blocks in the bag
4. The constraint is when moving from column `i` to `i+1`: we need the absolute difference between current height and next column's height to be ≤ `k`

- Increase height of column `i` by adding blocks
- Decrease height of column `i` by removing blocks

```python
def solve():
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # If already at the last column
    if n == 1:
        return "YES"
    
    bag = m
    
    # Process each transition from i to i+1
    for i in range(n - 1):
        # Current column i, next column i+1
        current_height = h[i]
        next_height = h[i + 1]
        
        # We need |current_height_adjusted - next_height| <= k
        # So current_height_adjusted should be in [next_height - k, next_height + k]
        
        # Calculate the minimum blocks needed
        if current_height <= next_height - k:
            # Need to make current_height smaller (remove blocks)
            # We need to reduce it to at most next_height - k
            # Since current_height <= next_height - k, we need to remove (current_height - (next_height - k)) blocks
            # Actually, if current_height < next_height - k, we need to remove (next_height - k - current_height) blocks
            # To reduce from current_height to (next_height - k), we need to remove (current_height - (next_height - k)) blocks?
            # No: if current_height = 2, next_height - k = 5, we need to reduce from 2 to 5? That's impossible!
            
            # Actually, current_height should be at least (next_height - k)
            # So if current_height < next_height - k, we need to make it bigger?
            # Wait, this is backwards.
            
            # If we want |adjusted - next_height| <= k, then adjusted should be in [next_height - k, next_height + k]
            # If current_height < next_height - k, we need to increase current_height to at least next_height - k
            # Cost = (next_height - k) - current_height
            cost_increase = (next_height - k) - current_height
            if cost_increase <= bag:
                bag -= cost_increase
            else:
                return "NO"
        elif current_height >= next_height + k:
            # Need to make current_height smaller (reduce to at most next_height + k)
            # Cost = current_height - (next_height + k)
            cost_decrease = current_height - (next_height + k)
            if cost_decrease <= bag:
                bag -= cost_decrease
            else:
                return "NO"
        else:
            # Already within range, no cost
            pass
    
    return "YES"

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

Wait, I think I have a logic error. Let me reconsider:

- If current_height < next_height - k: we need to increase current_height to at least (next_height - k)
- If current_height > next_height + k: we need to decrease current_height to at most (next_height + k)
- Otherwise, we're fine

And we can do this by adding blocks (to increase) or removing blocks (to decrease).

The cost:
- To increase: (next_height - k) - current_height (must be positive)
- To decrease: current_height - (next_height + k) (must be positive)

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        print("YES")
        continue
        
    bag = m
    
    # Process each transition from column i to i+1
    for i in range(n - 1):
        current_height = h[i]
        next_height = h[i + 1]
        
        # We need current_height_adjusted to be in [next_height - k, next_height + k]
        min_required = next_height - k
        max_required = next_height + k
        
        # If current_height is already in range, no change needed
        if current_height >= min_required and current_height <= max_required:
            continue
        elif current_height < min_required:
            # Need to increase current_height to at least min_required
            # Cost = min_required - current_height
            cost = min_required - current_height
            if bag < cost:
                print("NO")
                break
            bag -= cost
        elif current_height > max_required:
            # Need to decrease current_height to at most max_required
            # Cost = current_height - max_required
            cost = current_height - max_required
            if bag < cost:
                print("NO")
                break
            bag -= cost
    else:
        print("YES")
```

In the first example:

- We need to transition to column 2 with height 3.
- For transition 1->2: we need |h1 - 3| ≤ 1 → |h1 - 3| ≤ 1 → h1 ∈ [2,4]
- Currently h1=4 which is in [2,4], so no block cost needed.

But the example says it's possible. How?

They say: "take one block from the 1-st column, move to the 2-nd column, put the block on the 2-nd column, then move to the 3-rd column."

Ah! So when they take a block from column 1, they're reducing column 1's height to 3. Then they move to column 2 (which has height 3). Then they add a block to column 2 (so column 2 becomes 4). Then they move to column 3 (which has height 5).

So when they're at column 1, they can modify column 1 to height 3. Then when they move to column 2, they can modify column 2 to height 4.

So the adjustment happens at each column before moving to the next.

My algorithm should be:
- For each column i from 0 to n-2 (before moving to i+1):
  - Determine what height we need for column i to satisfy |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k
  - The required height is in [h_{i+1} - k, h_{i+1} + k]
  - We need to modify h_i to be within this range
  - The cost is the absolute difference between current h_i and the target height we choose
  - We should choose the target height that minimizes the cost (closest to current h_i)

But we can choose any height in [h_{i+1} - k, h_{i+1} + k]. The minimum cost is:
- If current h_i is within the range, cost = 0
- If current h_i < h_{i+1} - k, cost = (h_{i+1} - k) - h_i (to increase)
- If current h_i > h_{i+1} + k, cost = h_i - (h_{i+1} + k) (to decrease)

And we can do this by removing blocks (to decrease) or adding blocks (to increase).

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        print("YES")
        continue
        
    bag = m
    
    valid = True
    for i in range(n - 1):
        current_height = h[i]
        next_height = h[i + 1]
        
        # The required height for current column i is in [next_height - k, next_height + k]
        min_required = next_height - k
        max_required = next_height + k
        
        # If current height is already in range, no cost
        if current_height >= min_required and current_height <= max_required:
            continue
        elif current_height < min_required:
            # Need to increase current height to min_required
            cost = min_required - current_height
            if bag < cost:
                valid = False
                break
            bag -= cost
        elif current_height > max_required:
            # Need to decrease current height to max_required
            cost = current_height - max_required
            if bag < cost:
                valid = False
                break
            bag -= cost
            
    if valid:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

Oh! I had it backward!

Let me check: "remove one block from the top of the i-th column and put it in the bag" - so removing reduces column height, increases bag size.

"take one block out of the bag and place it on the top of the i-th column" - so adding increases column height, decreases bag size.

So:
- To decrease a column height: we remove a block, which increases the bag
- To increase a column height: we add a block, which decreases the bag

This changes everything!

For a transition from column i to i+1:
- If current_height is already in range [next_height - k, next_height + k], cost = 0
- If current_height < next_height - k: we need to increase the height of column i to at least (next_height - k)
- If current_height > next_height + k: we need to decrease the height of column i to at most (next_height + k)

Cost:
- To increase height: need to add blocks, which decreases the bag. So cost = (target - current_height), and bag -= cost
- To decrease height: need to remove blocks, which increases the bag. So bag += (current_height - target)

But removing blocks increases the bag, so we gain blocks.

Let me reframe:

For each transition:
- We can only change column i's height
- If we need to increase height (current < next - k): we need to add (next - k - current) blocks
  - This decreases our bag by (next - k - current)
- If we need to decrease height (current > next + k): we need to remove (current - (next + k)) blocks  
  - This increases our bag by (current - (next + k))

And we can only do this if our bag has enough blocks to add when we need to add.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        print("YES")
        continue
        
    bag = m
    
    valid = True
    for i in range(n - 1):
        current_height = h[i]
        next_height = h[i + 1]
        
        min_required = next_height - k
        max_required = next_height + k
        
        if current_height >= min_required and current_height <= max_required:
            # No change needed
            continue
        elif current_height < min_required:
            # Need to increase height to min_required
            blocks_needed = min_required - current_height
            if bag < blocks_needed:
                valid = False
                break
            bag -= blocks_needed
        elif current_height > max_required:
            # Need to decrease height to max_required
            blocks_removed = current_height - max_required
            bag += blocks_removed  # removing blocks increases bag size
            
    if valid:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

In my algorithm, I need to find the value of h_i to make it within [next_height - k, next_height + k], and the cost should be the minimum in terms of block operations.

For a given i, we can choose any value in [next_height - k, next_height + k] for h_i, and the cost is:
- If we increase: cost = target - current_height, and we lose that many blocks (bag -= cost)
- If we decrease: cost = current_height - target, and we gain that many blocks (bag += cost)

We want to minimize the impact on the final bag, or at least ensure we don't go negative.

But we might want to choose a value that allows future transitions.

However, since we only care about whether we can do it, and the future constraints only depend on the height of the column at the time, we can choose the value that minimizes the absolute difference, or that maximizes the bag size.

The best choice to maximize our bag might be to reduce when we have a choice, or increase when we have a choice.

But actually, for each transition, we have a choice of what to do with the current column.

The minimal cost to get to a value in the range is to choose the closest point in the range to current_height.

And that will minimize the block transfers.

So for each i, we should choose a value in [next_height - k, next_height + k] that is closest to current_height.

The distance is min(|current_height - x|) for x in [next_height - k, next_height + k].

This distance is:
- 0 if current_height in [next_height - k, next_height + k]
- |current_height - (next_height - k)| if current_height < next_height - k
- |current_height - (next_height + k)| if current_height > next_height + k

And we need to know if we can afford the blocks.

For the case where current_height < next_height - k:
- We need to increase to next_height - k
- cost = (next_height - k) - current_height
- this decreases bag by cost

For current_height > next_height + k:
- We need to decrease to next_height + k
- cost = current_height - (next_height + k)
- this increases bag by cost

And we need to ensure that if we are decreasing, the bag doesn't go negative, but we are increasing it, so it's safe.

In example 1:
- i=0: current=4, next=3, range=[2,4]
  - current=4 in [2,4] → cost=0, bag=0
- i=1: current=3, next=5, range=[4,6]
  - current=3 < 4 → need to increase to 4, cost=1, bag becomes -1 → impossible

But we can choose to reduce column 1 to 3 instead of 4.

When at column 1, we can choose any height in [2,4]. If we choose 3, then when we move to column 2, we have h2=3, and for transition to column 3, we need h2 in [4,6], so we need to increase to 4.

The cost is still 1 (bag needs to be >=1).

We start with bag=0, and to achieve this, we need to have at least 1 block at the time of adding.

But to have a block in the bag, we must have removed a block from a column.

When we reduce column 1 from 4 to 3, we remove 1 block, so bag becomes 1.

Then when at column 2, we add a block to increase from 3 to 4, bag becomes 0.

So in my algorithm, when at column 1, we are processing the transition to column 2, we should be able to reduce height to any value in [2,4].

The cost to reduce from 4 to 3 is 1, which increases the bag by 1.

So for the transition from i to i+1, if we need to reduce, the cost is the amount we remove, which increases the bag.

If we need to increase, the cost is the amount we add, which decreases the bag.

The key is that we are allowed to choose any value in the range, so we can choose one that might help future transitions.

In the future, when we are at column 2, we might need to increase, and if we have more blocks in the bag, it will be easier.

But since we only care about the final ability to make the transitions, and the only constraint is the bag size at the time of each operation, we need to ensure that at each step, when we need to increase, we have enough blocks in the bag.

The optimal strategy is to choose the value in the range that minimizes the cost, or maximizes the bag size if it helps.

For a given i, to maximize future flexibility, when we need to increase, we should do the minimum increase (to next_height - k), which minimizes the blocks we lose.

When we need to decrease, we should do the minimum decrease (to next_height + k), which increases the bag by as little as possible.

In other words, we should choose the closest point in the range.

This will minimize the absolute difference, and thus the block transfers.

So let's implement with the closest point:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        print("YES")
        continue
        
    bag = m
    
    valid = True
    for i in range(n - 1):
        current_height = h[i]
        next_height = h[i + 1]
        
        min_required = next_height - k
        max_required = next_height + k
        
        # Find the closest value in [min_required, max_required] to current_height
        if current_height >= min_required and current_height <= max_required:
            # No change needed
            continue
        elif current_height < min_required:
            # Need to increase to min_required
            blocks_needed = min_required - current_height
            if bag < blocks_needed:
                valid = False
                break
            bag -= blocks_needed
        elif current_height > max_required:
            # Need to decrease to max_required
            blocks_removed = current_height - max_required
            bag += blocks_removed
            
    if valid:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

Test with example 1:
- i=0: current=4, next=3, range=[2,4] - 4 in [2,4] → no change, bag=0
- i=1: current=3, next=5, range=[4,6] - 3 < 4 → need to increase to 4, blocks_needed=1, bag=0 < 1 → "NO"

In the algorithm, for each i, when we need to change the height, we should be able to choose any value in the range, and the net effect on the bag is:
- If we increase: bag decreases by the amount
- If we decrease: bag increases by the amount

And we can choose the value that allows us to have more blocks for future increases.

In particular, if we are at a column and we need to increase, we might want to increase as little as possible to save blocks for later.

If we need to decrease, we might want to decrease as little as possible to preserve blocks.

But in both cases, the net effect on the bag is determined by the amount we add or remove.

- The closest value to 4 is 4

The algorithm should allow us to choose the value in the range to maximize our bag for future needs.

Actually, we can only make decisions based on the current state.

Perhaps the correct insight is that for each column, the height can be adjusted to any value in [next_height - k, next_height + k], and the net effect on the bag is the amount of blocks we add or remove.

And we can choose the value to minimize the impact, or to maximize the bag if it helps.

Since we don't know the future, and the only constraint is that at each step when we need to increase, we must have enough blocks, the best we can do is to choose the value that minimizes the cost in terms of blocks lost.

But in the first example, at i=0, we are at column 1, and we can choose to reduce to 3, which increases bag by 1.

Then at i=1, we need to increase from 3 to 4, requiring 1 block, and we have bag=1, so it works.

So the algorithm should be: for each i, if we need to increase, we need to have enough blocks; if we need to decrease, we gain blocks.

And we can choose to decrease even if we don't need to, to gain blocks.

But we only need to make the transition possible.

So for a given i, what is the minimal blocks we need to have to make the transition?

We can choose any value in [min_required, max_required] for h_i.

The net effect on the bag is:
- If we increase from current_height to target: bag -= (target - current_height)
- If we decrease from current_height to target: bag += (current_height - target)

And we want to choose target to maximize the final bag if we have to increase later.

Since we don't know the future, and we only know the current constraints, the safest is to choose the closest point to minimize the block transfer.

In example 1 at i=0: current=4, next=3, range=[2,4]
- closest point is 4 (distance 0)
- so cost 0, bag=0

This is not optimal for future.

Perhaps we should choose a value that gives us more blocks for future increases.

If we reduce to 2: cost = 4-2 = 2, bag becomes 0+2 = 2
Then at i=1: need to increase from 2 to 4, cost=2, bag=2-2=0, which is enough.

If we reduce to 3: cost=1, bag=1, then at i=1: increase from 3 to 4, cost=1, bag=0.

If we keep at 4: cost=0, bag=0, at i=1: need cost=1, bag=0, not enough.

So to have a chance, we need to reduce to at least 3.

But how do we know to reduce?

The only way is to simulate all possibilities, but that's exponential.

Perhaps there is a simpler insight.

Another thought: the only constraint is that when we are at column i, we can adjust h_i to any value in [h_{i+1} - k, h_{i+1} + k], and we can do this by removing or adding blocks.

The net effect on the bag is the amount of blocks we add or remove.

We need to ensure that at each step when we need to increase, we have enough blocks in the bag.

For the future, if we reduce at a column, we gain blocks, which might help future increases.

To maximize the probability of success, we should reduce whenever possible to gain blocks.

In other words, if at any point we need to increase, and we have a choice to reduce (i.e., if current_height > next_height + k), then we should reduce to next_height + k to gain blocks.

If current_height < next_height - k, we must increase to next_height - k, because any value below that would require even more blocks to increase.

If current_height > next_height + k, we can choose to reduce to next_height + k to gain (current_height - (next_height + k)) blocks.

If current_height in range, no change.

So let's implement this:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        print("YES")
        continue
        
    bag = m
    
    valid = True
    for i in range(n - 1):
        current_height = h[i]
        next_height = h[i + 1]
        
        min_required = next_height - k
        max_required = next_height + k
        
        if current_height >= min_required and current_height <= max_required:
            # No change needed
            continue
        elif current_height < min_required:
            # Must increase to min_required
            blocks_needed = min_required - current_height
            if bag < blocks_needed:
                valid = False
                break
            bag -= blocks_needed
        elif current_height > max_required:
            # Can choose to reduce to max_required to gain blocks
            blocks_gained = current_height - max_required
            bag += blocks_gained
            
    if valid:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

Test with example 1: n=3, m=0, k=1, h=[4,3,5]
- i=0: current=4, next=3, range=[2,4] - 4 in [2,4] → no change, bag=0
- i=1: current=3, next=5, range=[4,6] - 3 < 4 → must increase to 4, blocks_needed=1, bag=0 < 1 → "NO"

Still not working.

I see the mistake. In example 1, at i=0, current=4, next=3, range=[2,4] - 4 is in [2,4], so no change, but we can choose to reduce to a lower value to gain blocks.

For the transition from 1 to 2, we can choose any value in [2,4] for h1. If we choose to reduce to 3, then at i=0, we reduce from 4 to 3, so we gain 1 block (bag becomes 1), and then at i=1, we need to increase from 3 to 4, which requires 1 block from bag.

So for the transition from i to i+1, if we reduce, we gain blocks.

The algorithm should be: if current_height < min_required, we must increase to min_required (because any value below min_required would require even more blocks to increase to a value in the range).

If current_height > max_required, we can choose to reduce to max_required to gain blocks.

If current_height in range, we can choose to reduce to a lower value to gain blocks, or increase to a higher value to lose blocks.

To maximize future flexibility, we should reduce to max_required if possible, or to a value that gives us blocks for future increases.

But in the case where current_height in range, we might want to reduce to max_required to gain blocks.

In example 1 at i=0: current=4, next=3, range=[2,4] - in range, so we can choose to reduce to any value in [2,4].

To maximize future flexibility, we should reduce to 3 (max_required is 4, so reduce to 3 is not to max_required).

We should reduce to a value that gives us blocks for future increases.

The best value to reduce to is as low as possible to gain as many blocks as possible, but we need to be in the range.

We can reduce to 2, which gives us 2 blocks, or to 3, which gives us 1 block.

For future, when at i=1, we need to increase from 3 to 4, so if we reduce to 2, at i=1 we need to increase from 2 to 4, cost=2, and we have bag=2, so it works.

So if we reduce to 2, we gain 2 blocks, and then at i=1, we increase from 2 to 4, cost=2, and we have bag=2, so it works.

In the algorithm, at each step, if we are in the range, we have a choice. To maximize future flexibility, we should reduce to the minimum possible value in the range, which is min_required.

If we reduce to min_required, we gain (current_height - min_required) blocks.

Then at future steps, we may have more blocks.

In the example, at i=0: current=4, min_required=2, so reduce to 2, gain 2 blocks, bag=0+2=2.

Then at i=1: current=2, next=5, range=[4,6] - 2 < 4, so need to increase to 4, cost=2, bag=2-2=0, which is enough.

So it works.

For a step where current_height in [min_required, max_required], the best choice is to reduce to min_required to gain (current_height - min_required) blocks.

For a step where current_height < min_required, we must increase to min_required, cost = min_required - current_height, bag -= cost.

For a step where current_height > max_required, we can reduce to max_required, cost = current_height - max_required, bag += cost.

For a step where current_height in range, we can reduce to min_required, bag += (current_height - min_required).

 Implementation:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        print("YES")
        continue
        
    bag = m
    valid = True
    
    for i in range(n - 1):
        current_height = h[i]
        next_height = h[i + 1]
        min_required = next_height - k
        max_required = next_height + k
        
        if current_height < min_required:
            # Must increase to min_required
            blocks_needed = min_required - current_height
            if bag < blocks_needed:
                valid = False
                break
            bag -= blocks_needed
        elif current_height > max_required:
            # Can choose to reduce to max_required to gain blocks
            blocks_gained = current_height - max_required
            bag += blocks_gained
        else:
            # current_height in [min_required, max_required]
            # To maximize future flexibility, reduce to min_required to gain blocks
            blocks_gained = current_height - min_required
            bag += blocks_gained
            
    if valid:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

```python
            if bag < blocks_needed:

            bag -= blocks_needed
        elif current_height > max_required:
            # Can reduce to max_required to gain blocks

            bag += blocks_gained
        else:
            # current_height is in [min_required, max_required]
            # Reduce to min_required to gain blocks
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Gildong is playing a video game called Block Adventure. In Block Adventure, there are n columns of blocks in a row, and the columns are numbered from 1 to n. All blocks have equal heights. The height of the i-th column is represented as h_i, which is the number of blocks stacked in the i-th column.

Gildong plays the game as a character that can stand only on the top of the columns. At the beginning, the character is standing on the top of the 1-st column. The goal of the game is to move the character to the top of the n-th column.

The character also has a bag that can hold infinitely many blocks. When the character is on the top of the i-th column, Gildong can take one of the following three actions as many times as he wants: 

  * if there is at least one block on the column, remove one block from the top of the i-th column and put it in the bag; 
  * if there is at least one block in the bag, take one block out of the bag and place it on the top of the i-th column; 
  * if i < n and |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k, move the character to the top of the i+1-st column. k is a non-negative integer given at the beginning of the game. Note that it is only possible to move to the next column. 



In actions of the first two types the character remains in the i-th column, and the value h_i changes.

The character initially has m blocks in the bag. Gildong wants to know if it is possible to win the game. Help Gildong find the answer to his question.

Input

Each test contains one or more test cases. The first line contains the number of test cases t (1 ≤ t ≤ 1000). Description of the test cases follows.

The first line of each test case contains three integers n, m, and k (1 ≤ n ≤ 100, 0 ≤ m ≤ 10^6, 0 ≤ k ≤ 10^6) — the number of columns in the game, the number of blocks in the character's bag at the beginning, and the non-negative integer k described in the statement.

The second line of each test case contains n integers. The i-th integer is h_i (0 ≤ h_i ≤ 10^6), the initial height of the i-th column.

Output

For each test case, print "YES" if it is possible to win the game. Otherwise, print "NO".

You can print each letter in any case (upper or lower).

Example

Input

5
3 0 1
4 3 5
3 1 2
1 4 7
4 10 0
10 20 10 20
2 5 5
0 11
1 9 9
99


Output

YES
NO
YES
NO
YES

Note

In the first case, Gildong can take one block from the 1-st column, move to the 2-nd column, put the block on the 2-nd column, then move to the 3-rd column.

In the second case, Gildong has to put the block in his bag on the 1-st column to get to the 2-nd column. But it is impossible to get to the 3-rd column because |h_2 - h_3| = 3 > k and there is no way to decrease the gap.

In the fifth case, the character is already on the n-th column from the start so the game is won instantly.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n columns of blocks, with heights h_i for column i
2. Character starts at column 1 and needs to reach column n
3. Three actions available:
   - Remove one block from current column (add to bag)
   - Add one block from bag to current column
   - Move to next column if |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k
4. We start with m blocks in the bag
5. We need to determine if it's possible to reach column n

## Key Insights

1. We can only move from column i to i+1 when |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k
2. We can modify the heights by adding or removing blocks (with the bag as a storage)
3. The critical constraint is that when we move from column i to i+1, the height difference must be ≤ k
4. We can always reduce a column's height (by removing blocks) to make it smaller
5. We can increase a column's height (by adding blocks from bag) to make it larger
6. But we have a limited bag capacity (initially m blocks)

## Strategy

For each consecutive pair of columns (i, i+1), we need to check if we can make |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k.

Since we can remove blocks from any column we're on, we can always reduce h_i or h_{i+1} (though we need to be careful about the bag).

The key insight: 
- We can always decrease the height of the current column (which we're standing on)
- We can only increase height by using blocks from our bag
- To make |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k, we can either:
  - Decrease h_i to make it smaller (if h_i > h_{i+1} + k)
  - Increase h_{i+1} to make it larger (if h_{i+1} < h_i - k)
  - Or make them closer by adjusting both

Actually, let's think more systematically:

When we're at column i, we can:
- Remove blocks from column i (decrease h_i)
- Add blocks to column i (increase h_i) - this uses blocks from our bag
- Move to column i+1 only if |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k

So for each transition from i to i+1, we need to ensure that after any modifications, |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k.

We can always reduce h_i to 0 (by removing all blocks), which means we can make the difference with h_{i+1} as h_{i+1}.

We can also increase h_i by adding blocks from our bag.

The constraint becomes: can we make |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k?

This means:
- Either h_{i+1} ≤ h_i + k (we can increase h_i)
- Or h_i ≤ h_{i+1} + k (we can decrease h_i)

But we need to consider the bag capacity.

## Detailed Analysis

For each transition from i to i+1:
1. If h_i ≥ h_{i+1} + k, we can reduce h_i to h_{i+1} + k (this requires removing (h_i - (h_{i+1} + k)) blocks from column i)
2. If h_i ≤ h_{i+1} - k, we can increase h_{i+1} to h_i + k (this requires adding (h_i + k - h_{i+1}) blocks to column i+1)

Actually, when we're at column i, we can:
- Remove blocks from column i
- Add blocks to column i (using bag)

To move to column i+1, we need |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k.

So we can modify h_i to any value ≥ 0, but we need to consider the blocks we can add from our bag.

The optimal strategy:
- To make |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k, we want h_{i+1} - h_i ≤ k and h_i - h_{i+1} ≤ k
- So we need |h_i - h_{i+1}| ≤ k

We can achieve this by:
1. If h_i > h_{i+1} + k: we need to reduce h_i to h_{i+1} + k (which requires removing (h_i - (h_{i+1} + k)) blocks from column i)
2. If h_i < h_{i+1} - k: we need to increase h_i to h_{i+1} - k (which requires adding (h_{i+1} - k - h_i) blocks to column i)

But wait - if h_i < h_{i+1} - k, we can't increase h_i to h_{i+1} - k because we're at column i. We can only add blocks to column i, making it larger.

So the condition is:
- If h_i > h_{i+1} + k: we need to remove (h_i - (h_{i+1} + k)) blocks from column i
- If h_i < h_{i+1} - k: we need to add (h_{i+1} - k - h_i) blocks to column i

Both operations can be done, but the bag constraint matters.

For the first transition (from column 1 to 2):
- We start with m blocks in bag
- We can remove blocks from column 1 (add to bag) or add blocks to column 1 (use bag)

But when we want to move from column 1 to 2, we need to check if we can make |h_1 - h_2| ≤ k.

The key insight is that we can always reduce h_1 to 0 (by removing all blocks), so if h_2 ≤ k, we can make the difference ≤ k.

If h_2 > h_1 + k, we need to remove (h_2 - h_1 - k) blocks from column 1.
